Just the same old Margaret.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of time when you felt your mood was noticeably different, like more high-spirited, unusually cheerful, or even more irritable than usual, along with this restlessness or increased activity you’ve described?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doctor, I can't say that my mood has been any different at all. I haven't been unusually cheerful or high-spirited, and I certainly haven't been more irritable. I mean, I'm just... well, I'm just my normal self! Like I've been trying to explain, it's just this physical restlessness, this need to keep moving and doing my chores over and over. It has nothing to do with my mood. \n\nI'm generally a very even-keeled person, you know. Arthur always appreciated that about me. He used to say, \"Margaret, you're as steady as a grandfather clock.\" And it's true! I don't get overly excited about things, and I don't let things get under my skin easily either. I remember when the neighbors next door—the Johnsons, lovely people, they moved to Florida a few years ago—when they got a new dog, a little terrier mix, and it would bark and bark at the fence. Susan was just beside herself, saying we should call the homeowners association, but I just said, \"Oh, let the poor thing bark, it's just getting used to its new yard.\" \n\nSo no, I haven't had any strange moods or anything like that. I feel perfectly fine in my spirits. I just can't sit still! I'll be sitting there, perfectly content, maybe listening to a nice classical program on the radio, and my legs will just start to jitter and I have to get up. Steady as a grandfather clock, just like Arthur said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or depressed, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms of depression that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. I know some people do—my sister-in-law, Beatrice, Arthur's younger sister, she used to get terrible spells in the wintertime. She called it her \"February gloom.\" She would just pull the curtains shut and stay in her bathrobe until three in the afternoon, barely eating a thing. We used to take her over a nice casserole, usually a tuna noodle or maybe a chicken divan, just to make sure she was getting a hot meal. But I never understood it, myself. \n\nI've just never been built that way. I've always found plenty to keep me occupied and cheerful. Even after Arthur passed—and that was a terrible blow, of course, a terrible blow, we had been married for fifty-two years—I didn't sink into a depression. I grieved, certainly, I missed him terribly, but I kept my routine. I kept the house clean, I kept going to my garden club on Thursdays... oh, the garden club ladies were such a comfort. We were preparing for the spring flower show right around that time, and focusing on my hydrangeas really gave me something to look forward to. \n\nSo, no, I've never lost interest in the things I enjoy. I still love my gardening, I still love keeping a nice home, I still enjoy reading my magazines and listening to the radio. I don't feel sad or hopeless. I don't have any of those symptoms you're talking about. It's just this silly restlessness, this inability to sit still in my armchair when all the chores are perfectly finished. But my spirits? Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here! I really don't mean to be difficult, I truly don't, but I thought we just went over this. No, I have never had a period of two weeks—or two days, or two hours—where I felt down or lost interest in my life. 

I mean, I'm eighty-seven years old, and I've seen a lot in my time, but I've always found joy in my daily routine. Even when I was a younger woman, raising the children and keeping the house while Arthur was working those long hours at the firm—he was a junior partner then, and they worked him to the bone, I hardly saw him before supper most nights—I never felt depressed. I had my hands full, of course, what with the laundry and the cooking and making sure the children had clean uniforms for school, but I enjoyed it. I took pride in it. 

And I still do! I still take pride in my home. Just yesterday, I spent an hour polishing the silver tea set. It didn't strictly need it, I suppose, I just did it last month, but my hands were itching to do something, and it gave me a lovely sense of accomplishment to see it shining on the sideboard. So no, I haven't lost interest in anything. I just... I just can't seem to stop doing them, even when they're already done. But my mood is perfectly fine. I promise you, doctor, I am not a depressed person.

If I'm not puttering around the kitchen reorganizing the pantry shelves—which I've done three times this month, by the way—I'm fixing the loose hinges on the cabinets, or arranging the photo albums, or signing up to bake another dozen cookies for the church bake sale. Even when I try to read the paper, my foot is tapping or I'm thinking about the weeds in the flower bed out front. It's just exhausting for everyone else, and honestly, a little exhausting for me too, but I just can't stop moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt unusually excited, overly energetic, or irritable, where you felt like your mood was significantly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I usually sit down in my favorite armchair, the blue floral one by the window, right around 8:30 after the evening news is over, and I can barely keep my eyes open. Susan always tells me I am nodding off, and I tell her I am just resting my eyes, but really, I am completely exhausted from being on my feet all day. \n\nI go to bed by 9:30 every single night, regular as clockwork. I have always been a good sleeper. Well, mostly, except when my late husband Arthur was alive and he used to snore so loudly, it was like a freight train in the bedroom, bless his heart. But no, I sleep a good seven or eight hours, and if I do not get that, oh my, I really feel it the next day. I am dragging my feet, I need an extra cup of coffee, maybe even a little nap in the afternoon, though I try not to take naps because there is always so much to get done around the house. \n\nSo to answer your question, no, I definitely do not feel rested if I only get a few hours. I need my full night of rest, absolutely. I am not one of those people who can just run on fumes, no sir, if I miss my sleep, I am just useless the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more involved in activities than usual, such as starting new projects, attending more events, or doing things more quickly or energetically than before?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before about the baking and the pantry, I am definitely keeping busy, but it's not really *more* activities or *new* projects, if you see what I mean. It's just the same old things I've always done—cleaning, organizing, church things—I just can't seem to stop doing them! \n\nI haven't suddenly taken up painting or decided to learn French or started going out dancing every night, heaven forbid. My friend Martha, she's 82, she just joined a water aerobics class and a book club all in the same week, and I told her, \"Martha, you're going to wear yourself to a frazzle!\" But me? No, I'm not starting anything new like that. I'm just puttering around my own house, doing the same chores I've done for sixty years, just... doing them over and over. \n\nLike the silver. I polished all my good silver last Tuesday. It's a beautiful set, Arthur bought it for our 25th anniversary, service for twelve, though goodness knows I haven't had twelve people over for dinner since the late nineties. But anyway, I polished it all on Tuesday, and then by Friday, I was sitting there, tapping my foot, looking at the silver chest, and I just had to get the polish out and do it all again. Not because it was tarnished, but because I just had to be doing *something* with my hands. \n\nSo it's not that I'm taking on the world or going to more events or anything grand like that. I'm not doing things faster, either—my hands are a bit stiff with arthritis, so I actually do things quite slowly these days. I just do them constantly. I'm just stuck in this loop of my regular chores because if I sit still, I feel like I'm going to jump right out of my skin. Susan says I'm like a wind-up toy that's stuck against a wall, just marching in place. So no, no new projects or big social calendars, just me and my broom and my silver polish, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested even if you haven't slept much, or that you need less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, I think we might have gotten our wires crossed, or maybe I didn't explain myself properly earlier! Like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my sleep hasn't changed a bit, and I certainly do not feel rested if I miss out on it. Goodness, no. \n\nIf I don't get my full night's rest, I am just absolutely useless the next day. I remember just last month, Susan came over and we stayed up watching one of those British mystery shows she likes so much—the ones with the lovely little stone cottages and the gardens, you know the kind, though I can never keep track of who the murderer is—and we didn't get to bed until nearly midnight! Well, let me tell you, the next morning I woke up at my usual 6:00 AM, because my body just wakes up then no matter what time I go to sleep, and I felt absolutely dreadful. I didn't feel rested in the slightest. My eyes were watering, my head felt like it was stuffed with cotton wool, and my joints were aching terribly. \n\nI tried to go about my day, I really did. I tried to do my dusting in the front room, but I just couldn't keep my usual pace. I had to sit right down at the kitchen table with a cup of hot tea and just stare out the window at the bird feeder for a good hour. So, no, I definitely do not have that thing where you can just run on empty. I need every single minute of my sleep, or I just fall apart. It's just during the day that I can't stop moving around and doing my chores, but once nighttime comes and my head hits that pillow, I am completely spent. Did you need me to write down my exact sleep schedule for your notes? I have a little notepad in my purse if that would help keep it straight...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing significantly more chores, tasks, or activities than you normally would, in a way that feels different from how you’ve been in the past?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness, doctor, I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit today! Like I mentioned when we were talking about the silver polish just a little while ago, I am not taking on *more* chores than I normally would, and it certainly doesn't feel like some big change from how I've always been. I have always, always been a meticulous housekeeper. Ever since Arthur and I bought our house in 1958—oh, it was a brand new build back then, beautiful hardwood floors that I swore I would never let get scuffed—I have kept a very strict routine. Mondays are for laundry, Tuesdays are for dusting, Wednesdays I do the floors... you get the picture. \n\nSo no, I'm not suddenly deciding to clean out the attic or reorganize the garage or anything out of the ordinary like that. It's not a new burst of energy where I'm doing *more* things. It's exactly the same list of chores I've had for decades. The only thing is, like I told you, I just can't seem to sit down and relax when the chores are done! So instead of doing something new, I just do the same old things again. \n\nI'll sweep the kitchen, put the broom away, sit down to read my Good Housekeeping magazine, and my legs just feel so jumpy and restless that I have to get up and sweep the kitchen all over again. Susan thinks I'm going to wear the finish right off the linoleum! But it's not a new activity, and I'm not doing *more* of them in a productive way, if that makes sense. I'm just sort of stuck repeating my usual routine because my body just won't let me sit still and be peaceful. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm answering you properly, since we seem to be going over the same ground again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts are racing or that you're talking more quickly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No racing thoughts, and I certainly don't think I'm talking any faster than I usually do. I've always been a bit of a talker, you see. Arthur used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey! He was always teasing me about it. We’d go to a dinner party—back when we used to do those sorts of things, oh, the sixties were a wonderful time for dinner parties, weren't they? I had this beautiful fondue pot... anyway, we’d go, and on the drive home, he’d just chuckle and say, \"Well, Margaret, you certainly kept the conversation lively tonight.\" \n\nBut no, my thoughts aren't racing. They just sort of hum along at their usual pace. I'm not having a million ideas popping into my head all at once, or feeling like my brain is moving too fast to keep up with. It's just my body that feels restless, like I was saying before with the sweeping and the silver. My mind is perfectly calm. In fact, sometimes when I'm polishing that silver for the second time in a week, my mind is completely blank! I'm just focusing on the little circles I'm making with the rag. \n\nAnd as for talking quickly, well, you can hear me right now. I just talk like I always have. Maybe I talk a little more than some people, but it's not a new thing, and it's certainly not because my mind is running away with me. It's just... well, it's just me! I like to explain things thoroughly so there are no misunderstandings. Like earlier, when I wanted to make sure you understood about my sleep schedule. I just think it's important to be clear, don't you? But no, no racing thoughts or fast talking here. Just the same old Margaret.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of time when you felt your mood was noticeably different, like more high-spirited, unusually cheerful, or even more irritable than usual, along with this restlessness or increased activity you’ve described?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doctor, I can't say that my mood has been any different at all. I haven't been unusually cheerful or high-spirited, and I certainly haven't been more irritable. I mean, I'm just... well, I'm just my normal self! Like I've been trying to explain, it's just this physical restlessness, this need to keep moving and doing my chores over and over. It has nothing to do with my mood. \n\nI'm generally a very even-keeled person, you know. Arthur always appreciated that about me. He used to say, \"Margaret, you're as steady as a grandfather clock.\" And it's true! I don't get overly excited about things, and I don't let things get under my skin easily either. I remember when the neighbors next door—the Johnsons, lovely people, they moved to Florida a few years ago—when they got a new dog, a little terrier mix, and it would bark and bark at the fence. Susan was just beside herself, saying we should call the homeowners association, but I just said, \"Oh, let the poor thing bark, it's just getting used to its new yard.\" \n\nSo no, I haven't had any strange moods or anything like that. I feel perfectly fine in my spirits. I just can't sit still! I'll be sitting there, perfectly content, maybe listening to a nice classical program on the radio, and my legs will just start to jitter and I have to get up. But I'm not upset about it, and I'm not thrilled about it either. It's just... bothersome, is what it is. Just bothersome. But my mood? My mood is exactly as it always is. Steady as a grandfather clock, just like Arthur said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or depressed, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms of depression that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. Depressed? Heavens, no. I've never been one to get down in the dumps like that. I know some people do—my sister-in-law, Beatrice, Arthur's younger sister, she used to get terrible spells in the wintertime. She called it her \"February gloom.\" She would just pull the curtains shut and stay in her bathrobe until three in the afternoon, barely eating a thing. We used to take her over a nice casserole, usually a tuna noodle or maybe a chicken divan, just to make sure she was getting a hot meal. But I never understood it, myself. \n\nI've just never been built that way. I've always found plenty to keep me occupied and cheerful. Even after Arthur passed—and that was a terrible blow, of course, a terrible blow, we had been married for fifty-two years—I didn't sink into a depression. I grieved, certainly, I missed him terribly, but I kept my routine. I kept the house clean, I kept going to my garden club on Thursdays... oh, the garden club ladies were such a comfort. We were preparing for the spring flower show right around that time, and focusing on my hydrangeas really gave me something to look forward to. \n\nSo, no, I've never lost interest in the things I enjoy. I still love my gardening, I still love keeping a nice home, I still enjoy reading my magazines and listening to the radio. I don't feel sad or hopeless. I don't have any of those symptoms you're talking about. It's just this silly restlessness, this inability to sit still in my armchair when all the chores are perfectly finished. But my spirits? No, my spirits are perfectly fine, doctor. I've always been a very practical, optimistic sort of person, and that certainly hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was clearly different from usual—like feeling unusually high, overly confident, or irritable—along with this restlessness or pressure to keep moving that you've described?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but no, I really haven't. My mood hasn't been different at all. I've never felt unusually high or... what was it? Overly confident? Goodness, no. I'm a very modest person, I like to think. I know my capabilities—I can bake a pie crust that flakes perfectly, and I can get a stain out of a white tablecloth like nobody's business—but I don't go around feeling, oh, grand or full of myself. \n\nAnd irritable? No, not irritable either. I told you about the Johnsons' dog, didn't I? I have a very long fuse, as Arthur used to say. It takes a lot to get me riled up. I just don't see the point in getting all bothered about things you can't control. \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any change in my mood to go along with this... this fidgeting. It's just my body that won't cooperate. I'll be sitting there, perfectly content in my mind, perfectly happy, and then my hands just start needing to do something. Or my feet start tapping. But inside? In my heart and my head? I'm just the same old Margaret. I really don't think my mood has anything to do with it at all. It's just a physical annoyance, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had even a single day when you felt unusually upbeat, restless, or irritable in a way that was noticeably different from your usual self—even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I truly don't know how else to say it, doctor, but no, not even for a single day. I've been sitting here trying to think back, really racking my brain, because I know you're trying to get to the bottom of this, and I want to be helpful, I really do. But I just don't have these mood swings you keep asking about. \n\nI don't have days where I wake up feeling unusually upbeat or on top of the world—I just wake up feeling like me. I have my cup of tea—Earl Grey, always, with just a splash of milk—and I read the morning paper, and I feel perfectly normal. And I don't have days where I'm suddenly irritable, either. I mean, goodness, if I was going to be irritable, it would have been last Tuesday when the paperboy threw the morning edition straight into my prize-winning azalea bush. Broke three branches right off! Susan was furious when she came over and saw it, she wanted to march right down to the distribution center. But I just said, \"Susan, it's a bush, it will grow back, and the poor boy was probably just in a rush to get to school.\" \n\nSo you see, even when things happen that *should* make a person irritable, I just don't get that way. I stay perfectly calm. It's just this... this nervous energy in my body, this need to sweep a floor that's already clean, or reorganize the pantry for the third time in a week. It's just my hands and my feet that won't settle. But my mood? No, not even for a day, not even for an hour. I'm just as steady as ever. I really don't think my mood is the problem here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even if it didn't happen recently?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here! I really don't mean to be difficult, I truly don't, but I thought we just went over this. No, I have never had a period of two weeks—or two days, or two hours—where I felt down or lost interest in my life. \n\nI mean, I'm eighty-seven years old, and I've seen a lot in my time, but I've always found joy in my daily routine. Even when I was a younger woman, raising the children and keeping the house while Arthur was working those long hours at the firm—he was a junior partner then, and they worked him to the bone, I hardly saw him before supper most nights—I never felt depressed. I had my hands full, of course, what with the laundry and the cooking and making sure the children had clean uniforms for school, but I enjoyed it. I took pride in it. \n\nAnd I still do! I still take pride in my home. Just yesterday, I spent an hour polishing the silver tea set. It didn't strictly need it, I suppose, I just did it last month, but my hands were itching to do something, and it gave me a lovely sense of accomplishment to see it shining on the sideboard. So no, I haven't lost interest in anything. I just... I just can't seem to stop doing them, even when they're already done. But my mood is perfectly fine. I promise you, doctor, I am not a depressed person.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Steady as a grandfather clock, just like Arthur said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or depressed, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms of depression that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. Depressed? Heavens, no. I've never been one to get down in the dumps like that. I know some people do—my sister-in-law, Beatrice, Arthur's younger sister, she used to get terrible spells in the wintertime. She called it her \"February gloom.\" She would just pull the curtains shut and stay in her bathrobe until three in the afternoon, barely eating a thing. We used to take her over a nice casserole, usually a tuna noodle or maybe a chicken divan, just to make sure she was getting a hot meal. But I never understood it, myself. \n\nI've just never been built that way. I've always found plenty to keep me occupied and cheerful. Even after Arthur passed—and that was a terrible blow, of course, a terrible blow, we had been married for fifty-two years—I didn't sink into a depression. I grieved, certainly, I missed him terribly, but I kept my routine. I kept the house clean, I kept going to my garden club on Thursdays... oh, the garden club ladies were such a comfort. We were preparing for the spring flower show right around that time, and focusing on my hydrangeas really gave me something to look forward to. \n\nSo, no, I've never lost interest in the things I enjoy. I still love my gardening, I still love keeping a nice home, I still enjoy reading my magazines and listening to the radio. I don't feel sad or hopeless. I don't have any of those symptoms you're talking about. It's just this silly restlessness, this inability to sit still in my armchair when all the chores are perfectly finished. But my spirits? No, my spirits are perfectly fine, doctor. I've always been a very practical, optimistic sort of person, and that certainly hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was clearly different from usual—like feeling unusually high, overly confident, or irritable—along with this restlessness or pressure to keep moving that you've described?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but no, I really haven't. My mood hasn't been different at all. I've never felt unusually high or... what was it? Overly confident? Goodness, no. I'm a very modest person, I like to think. I know my capabilities—I can bake a pie crust that flakes perfectly, and I can get a stain out of a white tablecloth like nobody's business—but I don't go around feeling, oh, grand or full of myself. \n\nAnd irritable? No, not irritable either. I told you about the Johnsons' dog, didn't I? I have a very long fuse, as Arthur used to say. It takes a lot to get me riled up. I just don't see the point in getting all bothered about things you can't control. \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any change in my mood to go along with this... this fidgeting. It's just my body that won't cooperate. I'll be sitting there, perfectly content in my mind, perfectly happy, and then my hands just start needing to do something. Or my feet start tapping. But inside? In my heart and my head? I'm just the same old Margaret. I really don't think my mood has anything to do with it at all. It's just a physical annoyance, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had even a single day when you felt unusually upbeat, restless, or irritable in a way that was noticeably different from your usual self—even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I truly don't know how else to say it, doctor, but no, not even for a single day. I've been sitting here trying to think back, really racking my brain, because I know you're trying to get to the bottom of this, and I want to be helpful, I really do. But I just don't have these mood swings you keep asking about. \n\nI don't have days where I wake up feeling unusually upbeat or on top of the world—I just wake up feeling like me. I have my cup of tea—Earl Grey, always, with just a splash of milk—and I read the morning paper, and I feel perfectly normal. And I don't have days where I'm suddenly irritable, either. I mean, goodness, if I was going to be irritable, it would have been last Tuesday when the paperboy threw the morning edition straight into my prize-winning azalea bush. Broke three branches right off! Susan was furious when she came over and saw it, she wanted to march right down to the distribution center. But I just said, \"Susan, it's a bush, it will grow back, and the poor boy was probably just in a rush to get to school.\" \n\nSo you see, even when things happen that *should* make a person irritable, I just don't get that way. I stay perfectly calm. It's just this... this nervous energy in my body, this need to sweep a floor that's already clean, or reorganize the pantry for the third time in a week. It's just my hands and my feet that won't settle. But my mood? No, not even for a day, not even for an hour. I'm just as steady as ever. I really don't think my mood is the problem here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even if it didn't happen recently?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here! I really don't mean to be difficult, I truly don't, but I thought we just went over this. No, I have never had a period of two weeks—or two days, or two hours—where I felt down or lost interest in my life. \n\nI mean, I'm eighty-seven years old, and I've seen a lot in my time, but I've always found joy in my daily routine. Even when I was a younger woman, raising the children and keeping the house while Arthur was working those long hours at the firm—he was a junior partner then, and they worked him to the bone, I hardly saw him before supper most nights—I never felt depressed. I had my hands full, of course, what with the laundry and the cooking and making sure the children had clean uniforms for school, but I enjoyed it. I took pride in it. \n\nAnd I still do! I still take pride in my home. Just yesterday, I spent an hour polishing the silver tea set. It didn't strictly need it, I suppose, I just did it last month, but my hands were itching to do something, and it gave me a lovely sense of accomplishment to see it shining on the sideboard. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, bless your heart, but I feel like a broken record! I really do. A different version of myself? Heavens, no. I have been exactly who I am for eighty-seven years, and I’ve never woken up feeling like a stranger in my own head. 

Now, you say more energy or activity... well, certainly, there are days when I get a bee in my bonnet and get a whole lot done. Why, just last month—or was it April? No, it was definitely last month because the tulips were just starting to droop out front—I decided the entire guest bedroom needed a deep clean. I took down the heavy drapes, which is no small task for a woman my age, let me tell you, and I scrubbed the baseboards until they absolutely shone. My daughter Susan came over and nearly had a fit. She said, "Mother, you have too much energy for your own good, you're going to break a hip doing all this!" 

But I wasn't unusually excited, and I certainly wasn't irritable or... or high. I was just... well, my hands and legs just wanted to go, go, go. It felt like if I sat down in my armchair to do my crossword puzzle, I’d just vibrate right out of the seat! So I put that physical energy into the baseboards instead of just tapping my foot to death. 

But my mood during all that? My mood was just plain old Margaret. I was happy as a clam, just wanting things tidy. I've never had these strange mood swings or bizarre excited spells you keep looking for, not even for a single afternoon. It really is just my body refusing to sit still, plain and simple.

My daughter keeps telling me, \"Mom, just sit down and watch your shows,\" but for the past year or so, I just can't seem to do it. I feel like I am constantly, and I mean constantly, on the go. If I'm not puttering around the kitchen reorganizing the pantry shelves—which I've done three times this month, by the way—I'm fixing the loose hinges on the cabinets, or arranging the photo albums, or signing up to bake another dozen cookies for the church bake sale. I just keep volunteering for one more thing, one more project. It's like I have this motor inside me that won't switch off, and I just have the hardest time sitting down and being still. Even when I try to read the paper, my foot is tapping or I'm thinking about the weeds in the flower bed out front. It's just exhausting for everyone else, and honestly, a little exhausting for me too, but I just can't stop moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt unusually excited, overly energetic, or irritable, where you felt like your mood was significantly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I mean, my mood is just... well, it's just me. I haven't felt like a different person or anything like that. I'm not running around feeling like I won the lottery or getting snappy with people out of the blue. \n\nWell, I say that, but maybe my daughter Susan thinks I'm a little irritable when she tries to take the broom out of my hands, but that's just because I'm right in the middle of sweeping the back porch and I want to finish it! You know how it is when you've finally got a good rhythm going and someone interrupts you. But no, inside, my mood is perfectly fine. I'm just my regular old self. \n\nI'm not unusually excited... well, I was a little excited when my youngest grandson got into the state college last month, but that's just normal grandmother excitement, you know? Not what you're talking about. I just feel exactly like myself, but a version of myself that just has to keep her hands and feet busy. It's really not my mood that's the problem at all. I wake up feeling like regular old me, I go to bed feeling like regular old me... I just happen to do a hundred little chores in between! So, no, nothing unusual with my moods.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need less sleep than usual lately, like feeling rested after only a few hours of sleep or not feeling tired even if you haven't slept much?\n\nPatient: Oh heavens no, I still need my sleep, just like I always have. In fact, by the time evening rolls around, after I have been running around doing all those things I mentioned—like the pantry and the baking and the sweeping—I am just absolutely tuckered out. I usually sit down in my favorite armchair, the blue floral one by the window, right around 8:30 after the evening news is over, and I can barely keep my eyes open. Susan always tells me I am nodding off, and I tell her I am just resting my eyes, but really, I am completely exhausted from being on my feet all day. \n\nI go to bed by 9:30 every single night, regular as clockwork. I have always been a good sleeper. Well, mostly, except when my late husband Arthur was alive and he used to snore so loudly, it was like a freight train in the bedroom, bless his heart. But no, I sleep a good seven or eight hours, and if I do not get that, oh my, I really feel it the next day. I am dragging my feet, I need an extra cup of coffee, maybe even a little nap in the afternoon, though I try not to take naps because there is always so much to get done around the house. \n\nSo to answer your question, no, I definitely do not feel rested if I only get a few hours. I need my full night of rest, absolutely. I am not one of those people who can just run on fumes, no sir, if I miss my sleep, I am just useless the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more involved in activities than usual, such as starting new projects, attending more events, or doing things more quickly or energetically than before?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before about the baking and the pantry, I am definitely keeping busy, but it's not really *more* activities or *new* projects, if you see what I mean. It's just the same old things I've always done—cleaning, organizing, church things—I just can't seem to stop doing them! \n\nI haven't suddenly taken up painting or decided to learn French or started going out dancing every night, heaven forbid. My friend Martha, she's 82, she just joined a water aerobics class and a book club all in the same week, and I told her, \"Martha, you're going to wear yourself to a frazzle!\" But me? No, I'm not starting anything new like that. I'm just puttering around my own house, doing the same chores I've done for sixty years, just... doing them over and over. \n\nLike the silver. I polished all my good silver last Tuesday. It's a beautiful set, Arthur bought it for our 25th anniversary, service for twelve, though goodness knows I haven't had twelve people over for dinner since the late nineties. But anyway, I polished it all on Tuesday, and then by Friday, I was sitting there, tapping my foot, looking at the silver chest, and I just had to get the polish out and do it all again. Not because it was tarnished, but because I just had to be doing *something* with my hands. \n\nSo it's not that I'm taking on the world or going to more events or anything grand like that. I'm not doing things faster, either—my hands are a bit stiff with arthritis, so I actually do things quite slowly these days. I just do them constantly. I'm just stuck in this loop of my regular chores because if I sit still, I feel like I'm going to jump right out of my skin. Susan says I'm like a wind-up toy that's stuck against a wall, just marching in place. So no, no new projects or big social calendars, just me and my broom and my silver polish, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested even if you haven't slept much, or that you need less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, I think we might have gotten our wires crossed, or maybe I didn't explain myself properly earlier! Like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my sleep hasn't changed a bit, and I certainly do not feel rested if I miss out on it. Goodness, no. \n\nIf I don't get my full night's rest, I am just absolutely useless the next day. I remember just last month, Susan came over and we stayed up watching one of those British mystery shows she likes so much—the ones with the lovely little stone cottages and the gardens, you know the kind, though I can never keep track of who the murderer is—and we didn't get to bed until nearly midnight! Well, let me tell you, the next morning I woke up at my usual 6:00 AM, because my body just wakes up then no matter what time I go to sleep, and I felt absolutely dreadful. I didn't feel rested in the slightest. My eyes were watering, my head felt like it was stuffed with cotton wool, and my joints were aching terribly. \n\nI tried to go about my day, I really did. I tried to do my dusting in the front room, but I just couldn't keep my usual pace. I had to sit right down at the kitchen table with a cup of hot tea and just stare out the window at the bird feeder for a good hour. So, no, I definitely do not have that thing where you can just run on empty. I need every single minute of my sleep, or I just fall apart. It's just during the day that I can't stop moving around and doing my chores, but once nighttime comes and my head hits that pillow, I am completely spent. Did you need me to write down my exact sleep schedule for your notes? I have a little notepad in my purse if that would help keep it straight...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing significantly more chores, tasks, or activities than you normally would, in a way that feels different from how you’ve been in the past?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness, doctor, I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit today! Like I mentioned when we were talking about the silver polish just a little while ago, I am not taking on *more* chores than I normally would, and it certainly doesn't feel like some big change from how I've always been. I have always, always been a meticulous housekeeper. Ever since Arthur and I bought our house in 1958—oh, it was a brand new build back then, beautiful hardwood floors that I swore I would never let get scuffed—I have kept a very strict routine. Mondays are for laundry, Tuesdays are for dusting, Wednesdays I do the floors... you get the picture. \n\nSo no, I'm not suddenly deciding to clean out the attic or reorganize the garage or anything out of the ordinary like that. It's not a new burst of energy where I'm doing *more* things. It's exactly the same list of chores I've had for decades. The only thing is, like I told you, I just can't seem to sit down and relax when the chores are done! So instead of doing something new, I just do the same old things again. \n\nI'll sweep the kitchen, put the broom away, sit down to read my Good Housekeeping magazine, and my legs just feel so jumpy and restless that I have to get up and sweep the kitchen all over again. Susan thinks I'm going to wear the finish right off the linoleum! But it's not a new activity, and I'm not doing *more* of them in a productive way, if that makes sense. I'm just sort of stuck repeating my usual routine because my body just won't let me sit still and be peaceful. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm answering you properly, since we seem to be going over the same ground again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts are racing or that you're talking more quickly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No racing thoughts, and I certainly don't think I'm talking any faster than I usually do. I've always been a bit of a talker, you see. Arthur used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey! He was always teasing me about it. We’d go to a dinner party—back when we used to do those sorts of things, oh, the sixties were a wonderful time for dinner parties, weren't they? I had this beautiful fondue pot... anyway, we’d go, and on the drive home, he’d just chuckle and say, \"Well, Margaret, you certainly kept the conversation lively tonight.\" \n\nBut no, my thoughts aren't racing. They just sort of hum along at their usual pace. I'm not having a million ideas popping into my head all at once, or feeling like my brain is moving too fast to keep up with. It's just my body that feels restless, like I was saying before with the sweeping and the silver. My mind is perfectly calm. In fact, sometimes when I'm polishing that silver for the second time in a week, my mind is completely blank! I'm just focusing on the little circles I'm making with the rag. \n\nAnd as for talking quickly, well, you can hear me right now. I just talk like I always have. Maybe I talk a little more than some people, but it's not a new thing, and it's certainly not because my mind is running away with me. It's just... well, it's just me! I like to explain things thoroughly so there are no misunderstandings. Like earlier, when I wanted to make sure you understood about my sleep schedule. I just think it's important to be clear, don't you? But no, no racing thoughts or fast talking here. Just the same old Margaret.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of time when you felt your mood was noticeably different, like more high-spirited, unusually cheerful, or even more irritable than usual, along with this restlessness or increased activity you’ve described?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doctor, I can't say that my mood has been any different at all. I haven't been unusually cheerful or high-spirited, and I certainly haven't been more irritable. I mean, I'm just... well, I'm just my normal self! Like I've been trying to explain, it's just this physical restlessness, this need to keep moving and doing my chores over and over. It has nothing to do with my mood. \n\nI'm generally a very even-keeled person, you know. Arthur always appreciated that about me. He used to say, \"Margaret, you're as steady as a grandfather clock.\" And it's true! I don't get overly excited about things, and I don't let things get under my skin easily either. I remember when the neighbors next door—the Johnsons, lovely people, they moved to Florida a few years ago—when they got a new dog, a little terrier mix, and it would bark and bark at the fence. Susan was just beside herself, saying we should call the homeowners association, but I just said, \"Oh, let the poor thing bark, it's just getting used to its new yard.\" \n\nSo no, I haven't had any strange moods or anything like that. I feel perfectly fine in my spirits. I just can't sit still! I'll be sitting there, perfectly content, maybe listening to a nice classical program on the radio, and my legs will just start to jitter and I have to get up. But I'm not upset about it, and I'm not thrilled about it either. It's just... bothersome, is what it is. Just bothersome. But my mood? My mood is exactly as it always is. Steady as a grandfather clock, just like Arthur said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or depressed, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms of depression that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. Depressed? Heavens, no. I've never been one to get down in the dumps like that. I know some people do—my sister-in-law, Beatrice, Arthur's younger sister, she used to get terrible spells in the wintertime. She called it her \"February gloom.\" She would just pull the curtains shut and stay in her bathrobe until three in the afternoon, barely eating a thing. We used to take her over a nice casserole, usually a tuna noodle or maybe a chicken divan, just to make sure she was getting a hot meal. But I never understood it, myself. \n\nI've just never been built that way. I've always found plenty to keep me occupied and cheerful. Even after Arthur passed—and that was a terrible blow, of course, a terrible blow, we had been married for fifty-two years—I didn't sink into a depression. I grieved, certainly, I missed him terribly, but I kept my routine. I kept the house clean, I kept going to my garden club on Thursdays... oh, the garden club ladies were such a comfort. We were preparing for the spring flower show right around that time, and focusing on my hydrangeas really gave me something to look forward to. \n\nSo, no, I've never lost interest in the things I enjoy. I still love my gardening, I still love keeping a nice home, I still enjoy reading my magazines and listening to the radio. I don't feel sad or hopeless. I don't have any of those symptoms you're talking about. It's just this silly restlessness, this inability to sit still in my armchair when all the chores are perfectly finished. But my spirits? No, my spirits are perfectly fine, doctor. I've always been a very practical, optimistic sort of person, and that certainly hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was clearly different from usual—like feeling unusually high, overly confident, or irritable—along with this restlessness or pressure to keep moving that you've described?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but no, I really haven't. My mood hasn't been different at all. I've never felt unusually high or... what was it? Overly confident? Goodness, no. I'm a very modest person, I like to think. I know my capabilities—I can bake a pie crust that flakes perfectly, and I can get a stain out of a white tablecloth like nobody's business—but I don't go around feeling, oh, grand or full of myself. \n\nAnd irritable? No, not irritable either. I told you about the Johnsons' dog, didn't I? I have a very long fuse, as Arthur used to say. It takes a lot to get me riled up. I just don't see the point in getting all bothered about things you can't control. \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any change in my mood to go along with this... this fidgeting. It's just my body that won't cooperate. I'll be sitting there, perfectly content in my mind, perfectly happy, and then my hands just start needing to do something. Or my feet start tapping. But inside? In my heart and my head? I'm just the same old Margaret. I really don't think my mood has anything to do with it at all. It's just a physical annoyance, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had even a single day when you felt unusually upbeat, restless, or irritable in a way that was noticeably different from your usual self—even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I truly don't know how else to say it, doctor, but no, not even for a single day. I've been sitting here trying to think back, really racking my brain, because I know you're trying to get to the bottom of this, and I want to be helpful, I really do. But I just don't have these mood swings you keep asking about. \n\nI don't have days where I wake up feeling unusually upbeat or on top of the world—I just wake up feeling like me. I have my cup of tea—Earl Grey, always, with just a splash of milk—and I read the morning paper, and I feel perfectly normal. And I don't have days where I'm suddenly irritable, either. I mean, goodness, if I was going to be irritable, it would have been last Tuesday when the paperboy threw the morning edition straight into my prize-winning azalea bush. Broke three branches right off! Susan was furious when she came over and saw it, she wanted to march right down to the distribution center. But I just said, \"Susan, it's a bush, it will grow back, and the poor boy was probably just in a rush to get to school.\" \n\nSo you see, even when things happen that *should* make a person irritable, I just don't get that way. I stay perfectly calm. It's just this... this nervous energy in my body, this need to sweep a floor that's already clean, or reorganize the pantry for the third time in a week. It's just my hands and my feet that won't settle. But my mood? No, not even for a day, not even for an hour. I'm just as steady as ever. I really don't think my mood is the problem here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even if it didn't happen recently?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here! I really don't mean to be difficult, I truly don't, but I thought we just went over this. No, I have never had a period of two weeks—or two days, or two hours—where I felt down or lost interest in my life. \n\nI mean, I'm eighty-seven years old, and I've seen a lot in my time, but I've always found joy in my daily routine. Even when I was a younger woman, raising the children and keeping the house while Arthur was working those long hours at the firm—he was a junior partner then, and they worked him to the bone, I hardly saw him before supper most nights—I never felt depressed. I had my hands full, of course, what with the laundry and the cooking and making sure the children had clean uniforms for school, but I enjoyed it. I took pride in it. \n\nAnd I still do! I still take pride in my home. Just yesterday, I spent an hour polishing the silver tea set. It didn't strictly need it, I suppose, I just did it last month, but my hands were itching to do something, and it gave me a lovely sense of accomplishment to see it shining on the sideboard. So no, I haven't lost interest in anything. I just... I just can't seem to stop doing them, even when they're already done. But my mood is perfectly fine. I promise you, doctor, I am not a depressed person.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had even a short period—maybe just a few days—when you felt like a noticeably different version of yourself, with a mood that was unusually high, excited, or irritable, along with more energy or activity than usual?\n\nPatient: Doctor, bless your heart, but I feel like a broken record! I really do. A different version of myself? Heavens, no. I have been exactly who I am for eighty-seven years, and I’ve never woken up feeling like a stranger in my own head. \n\nNow, you say more energy or activity... well, certainly, there are days when I get a bee in my bonnet and get a whole lot done. Why, just last month—or was it April? No, it was definitely last month because the tulips were just starting to droop out front—I decided the entire guest bedroom needed a deep clean. I took down the heavy drapes, which is no small task for a woman my age, let me tell you, and I scrubbed the baseboards until they absolutely shone. My daughter Susan came over and nearly had a fit. She said, \"Mother, you have too much energy for your own good, you're going to break a hip doing all this!\" \n\nBut I wasn't unusually excited, and I certainly wasn't irritable or... or high. I was just... well, my hands and legs just wanted to go, go, go. It felt like if I sat down in my armchair to do my crossword puzzle, I’d just vibrate right out of the seat! So I put that physical energy into the baseboards instead of just tapping my foot to death. \n\nBut my mood during all that? My mood was just plain old Margaret. I was happy as a clam, just wanting things tidy. I've never had these strange mood swings or bizarre excited spells you keep looking for, not even for a single afternoon. It really is just my body refusing to sit still, plain and simple.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I haven't been unusually cheerful or high-spirited, and I certainly haven't been more irritable. I mean, I'm just... well, I'm just my normal self! Like I've been trying to explain, it's just this physical restlessness, this need to keep moving and doing my chores over and over. It has nothing to do with my mood. \n\nI'm generally a very even-keeled person, you know. Arthur always appreciated that about me. He used to say, \"Margaret, you're as steady as a grandfather clock.\" And it's true! I don't get overly excited about things, and I don't let things get under my skin easily either. I remember when the neighbors next door—the Johnsons, lovely people, they moved to Florida a few years ago—when they got a new dog, a little terrier mix, and it would bark and bark at the fence. Susan was just beside herself, saying we should call the homeowners association, but I just said, \"Oh, let the poor thing bark, it's just getting used to its new yard.\" \n\nSo no, I haven't had any strange moods or anything like that. I feel perfectly fine in my spirits. I just can't sit still! I'll be sitting there, perfectly content, maybe listening to a nice classical program on the radio, and my legs will just start to jitter and I have to get up. But I'm not upset about it, and I'm not thrilled about it either. It's just... bothersome, is what it is. Just bothersome. But my mood? My mood is exactly as it always is. Steady as a grandfather clock, just like Arthur said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or depressed, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms of depression that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. Depressed? Heavens, no. I've never been one to get down in the dumps like that. I know some people do—my sister-in-law, Beatrice, Arthur's younger sister, she used to get terrible spells in the wintertime. She called it her \"February gloom.\" She would just pull the curtains shut and stay in her bathrobe until three in the afternoon, barely eating a thing. We used to take her over a nice casserole, usually a tuna noodle or maybe a chicken divan, just to make sure she was getting a hot meal. But I never understood it, myself. \n\nI've just never been built that way. I've always found plenty to keep me occupied and cheerful. Even after Arthur passed—and that was a terrible blow, of course, a terrible blow, we had been married for fifty-two years—I didn't sink into a depression. I grieved, certainly, I missed him terribly, but I kept my routine. I kept the house clean, I kept going to my garden club on Thursdays... oh, the garden club ladies were such a comfort. We were preparing for the spring flower show right around that time, and focusing on my hydrangeas really gave me something to look forward to. \n\nSo, no, I've never lost interest in the things I enjoy. I still love my gardening, I still love keeping a nice home, I still enjoy reading my magazines and listening to the radio. I don't feel sad or hopeless. I don't have any of those symptoms you're talking about. It's just this silly restlessness, this inability to sit still in my armchair when all the chores are perfectly finished. But my spirits? No, my spirits are perfectly fine, doctor. I've always been a very practical, optimistic sort of person, and that certainly hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was clearly different from usual—like feeling unusually high, overly confident, or irritable—along with this restlessness or pressure to keep moving that you've described?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but no, I really haven't. My mood hasn't been different at all. I've never felt unusually high or... what was it? Overly confident? Goodness, no. I'm a very modest person, I like to think. I know my capabilities—I can bake a pie crust that flakes perfectly, and I can get a stain out of a white tablecloth like nobody's business—but I don't go around feeling, oh, grand or full of myself. \n\nAnd irritable? No, not irritable either. I told you about the Johnsons' dog, didn't I? I have a very long fuse, as Arthur used to say. It takes a lot to get me riled up. I just don't see the point in getting all bothered about things you can't control. \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any change in my mood to go along with this... this fidgeting. It's just my body that won't cooperate. I'll be sitting there, perfectly content in my mind, perfectly happy, and then my hands just start needing to do something. Or my feet start tapping. But inside? In my heart and my head? I'm just the same old Margaret. I really don't think my mood has anything to do with it at all. It's just a physical annoyance, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had even a single day when you felt unusually upbeat, restless, or irritable in a way that was noticeably different from your usual self—even if it didn’t last long?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I truly don't know how else to say it, doctor, but no, not even for a single day. I've been sitting here trying to think back, really racking my brain, because I know you're trying to get to the bottom of this, and I want to be helpful, I really do. But I just don't have these mood swings you keep asking about. \n\nI don't have days where I wake up feeling unusually upbeat or on top of the world—I just wake up feeling like me. I have my cup of tea—Earl Grey, always, with just a splash of milk—and I read the morning paper, and I feel perfectly normal. And I don't have days where I'm suddenly irritable, either. I mean, goodness, if I was going to be irritable, it would have been last Tuesday when the paperboy threw the morning edition straight into my prize-winning azalea bush. Broke three branches right off! Susan was furious when she came over and saw it, she wanted to march right down to the distribution center. But I just said, \"Susan, it's a bush, it will grow back, and the poor boy was probably just in a rush to get to school.\" \n\nSo you see, even when things happen that *should* make a person irritable, I just don't get that way. I stay perfectly calm. It's just this... this nervous energy in my body, this need to sweep a floor that's already clean, or reorganize the pantry for the third time in a week. It's just my hands and my feet that won't settle. But my mood? No, not even for a day, not even for an hour. I'm just as steady as ever. I really don't think my mood is the problem here at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even if it didn't happen recently?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we're going around in circles here! I really don't mean to be difficult, I truly don't, but I thought we just went over this. No, I have never had a period of two weeks—or two days, or two hours—where I felt down or lost interest in my life. \n\nI mean, I'm eighty-seven years old, and I've seen a lot in my time, but I've always found joy in my daily routine. Even when I was a younger woman, raising the children and keeping the house while Arthur was working those long hours at the firm—he was a junior partner then, and they worked him to the bone, I hardly saw him before supper most nights—I never felt depressed. I had my hands full, of course, what with the laundry and the cooking and making sure the children had clean uniforms for school, but I enjoyed it. I took pride in it. \n\nAnd I still do! I still take pride in my home. Just yesterday, I spent an hour polishing the silver tea set. It didn't strictly need it, I suppose, I just did it last month, but my hands were itching to do something, and it gave me a lovely sense of accomplishment to see it shining on the sideboard. So no, I haven't lost interest in anything. I just... I just can't seem to stop doing them, even when they're already done. But my mood is perfectly fine. I promise you, doctor, I am not a depressed person.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had even a short period—maybe just a few days—when you felt like a noticeably different version of yourself, with a mood that was unusually high, excited, or irritable, along with more energy or activity than usual?\n\nPatient: Doctor, bless your heart, but I feel like a broken record! I really do. A different version of myself? Heavens, no. I have been exactly who I am for eighty-seven years, and I’ve never woken up feeling like a stranger in my own head. \n\nNow, you say more energy or activity... well, certainly, there are days when I get a bee in my bonnet and get a whole lot done. Why, just last month—or was it April? No, it was definitely last month because the tulips were just starting to droop out front—I decided the entire guest bedroom needed a deep clean. I took down the heavy drapes, which is no small task for a woman my age, let me tell you, and I scrubbed the baseboards until they absolutely shone. My daughter Susan came over and nearly had a fit. She said, \"Mother, you have too much energy for your own good, you're going to break a hip doing all this!\" \n\nBut I wasn't unusually excited, and I certainly wasn't irritable or... or high. I was just... well, my hands and legs just wanted to go, go, go. It felt like if I sat down in my armchair to do my crossword puzzle, I’d just vibrate right out of the seat! So I put that physical energy into the baseboards instead of just tapping my foot to death. \n\nBut my mood during all that? My mood was just plain old Margaret. I was happy as a clam, just wanting things tidy. I've never had these strange mood swings or bizarre excited spells you keep looking for, not even for a single afternoon. It really is just my body refusing to sit still, plain and simple.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Doctor, I... I really don't know how many different ways I can say this. I feel like I'm repeating myself until I'm blue in the face! Are you quite sure you're listening to me? Because we just talked about this, not five minutes ago! 

No, I have never had a single day where I felt unusually upbeat or irritable or restless in my *mind*. I've never felt like a different person. I am Margaret. I have always been Margaret. I wake up, I have my Earl Grey, I read the paper, and I feel exactly the same way I have felt for the last eighty-seven years. 

Now, my body, my hands and my feet, yes, they are restless. They won't stop moving. I told you about the baseboards, I told you about the silver tea set, I told you about sweeping the floor when it's already perfectly clean. But that is not my mood! That is just my body having too much nervous energy. It's like a motor running inside me that won't shut off, but my mind, my feelings... they are perfectly steady. 

I'm not irritable, either. I told you about the paperboy and the azalea bush! If I didn't get irritable then, when my prize-winning bush was ruined, why on earth would I get irritable for no reason at all? I just don't have these mood problems you keep asking about. I really, truly don't. I'm a very even-keeled person, doctor. Always have been.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.3, F31.81, F31.89\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's just... it's a bit hard to explain, really, because you'd think at my age—I'm 87, you know—you'd think I'd be slowing down and enjoying a bit of rest. My daughter keeps telling me, \"Mom, just sit down and watch your shows,\" but for the past year or so, I just can't seem to do it. I feel like I am constantly, and I mean constantly, on the go. If I'm not puttering around the kitchen reorganizing the pantry shelves—which I've done three times this month, by the way—I'm fixing the loose hinges on the cabinets, or arranging the photo albums, or signing up to bake another dozen cookies for the church bake sale. I just keep volunteering for one more thing, one more project. It's like I have this motor inside me that won't switch off, and I just have the hardest time sitting down and being still. Even when I try to read the paper, my foot is tapping or I'm thinking about the weeds in the flower bed out front. It's just exhausting for everyone else, and honestly, a little exhausting for me too, but I just can't stop moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt unusually excited, overly energetic, or irritable, where you felt like your mood was significantly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. In fact, by the time evening rolls around, after I have been running around doing all those things I mentioned—like the pantry and the baking and the sweeping—I am just absolutely tuckered out. I usually sit down in my favorite armchair, the blue floral one by the window, right around 8:30 after the evening news is over, and I can barely keep my eyes open. Susan always tells me I am nodding off, and I tell her I am just resting my eyes, but really, I am completely exhausted from being on my feet all day. \n\nI go to bed by 9:30 every single night, regular as clockwork. I have always been a good sleeper. Well, mostly, except when my late husband Arthur was alive and he used to snore so loudly, it was like a freight train in the bedroom, bless his heart. But no, I sleep a good seven or eight hours, and if I do not get that, oh my, I really feel it the next day. I am dragging my feet, I need an extra cup of coffee, maybe even a little nap in the afternoon, though I try not to take naps because there is always so much to get done around the house. \n\nSo to answer your question, no, I definitely do not feel rested if I only get a few hours. I need my full night of rest, absolutely. I am not one of those people who can just run on fumes, no sir, if I miss my sleep, I am just useless the next day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more involved in activities than usual, such as starting new projects, attending more events, or doing things more quickly or energetically than before?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before about the baking and the pantry, I am definitely keeping busy, but it's not really *more* activities or *new* projects, if you see what I mean. It's just the same old things I've always done—cleaning, organizing, church things—I just can't seem to stop doing them! \n\nI haven't suddenly taken up painting or decided to learn French or started going out dancing every night, heaven forbid. My friend Martha, she's 82, she just joined a water aerobics class and a book club all in the same week, and I told her, \"Martha, you're going to wear yourself to a frazzle!\" But me? No, I'm not starting anything new like that. I'm just puttering around my own house, doing the same chores I've done for sixty years, just... doing them over and over. \n\nLike the silver. I polished all my good silver last Tuesday. It's a beautiful set, Arthur bought it for our 25th anniversary, service for twelve, though goodness knows I haven't had twelve people over for dinner since the late nineties. But anyway, I polished it all on Tuesday, and then by Friday, I was sitting there, tapping my foot, looking at the silver chest, and I just had to get the polish out and do it all again. Not because it was tarnished, but because I just had to be doing *something* with my hands. \n\nSo it's not that I'm taking on the world or going to more events or anything grand like that. I'm not doing things faster, either—my hands are a bit stiff with arthritis, so I actually do things quite slowly these days. I just do them constantly. I'm just stuck in this loop of my regular chores because if I sit still, I feel like I'm going to jump right out of my skin. Susan says I'm like a wind-up toy that's stuck against a wall, just marching in place. So no, no new projects or big social calendars, just me and my broom and my silver polish, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested even if you haven't slept much, or that you need less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, doctor, I think we might have gotten our wires crossed, or maybe I didn't explain myself properly earlier! Like I was saying just a few minutes ago, my sleep hasn't changed a bit, and I certainly do not feel rested if I miss out on it. Goodness, no. \n\nIf I don't get my full night's rest, I am just absolutely useless the next day. I remember just last month, Susan came over and we stayed up watching one of those British mystery shows she likes so much—the ones with the lovely little stone cottages and the gardens, you know the kind, though I can never keep track of who the murderer is—and we didn't get to bed until nearly midnight! Well, let me tell you, the next morning I woke up at my usual 6:00 AM, because my body just wakes up then no matter what time I go to sleep, and I felt absolutely dreadful. I didn't feel rested in the slightest. My eyes were watering, my head felt like it was stuffed with cotton wool, and my joints were aching terribly. \n\nI tried to go about my day, I really did. I tried to do my dusting in the front room, but I just couldn't keep my usual pace. I had to sit right down at the kitchen table with a cup of hot tea and just stare out the window at the bird feeder for a good hour. It's just during the day that I can't stop moving around and doing my chores, but once nighttime comes and my head hits that pillow, I am completely spent. Did you need me to write down my exact sleep schedule for your notes? I have a little notepad in my purse if that would help keep it straight...\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing significantly more chores, tasks, or activities than you normally would, in a way that feels different from how you’ve been in the past?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness, doctor, I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit today! Like I mentioned when we were talking about the silver polish just a little while ago, I am not taking on *more* chores than I normally would, and it certainly doesn't feel like some big change from how I've always been. I have always, always been a meticulous housekeeper. Ever since Arthur and I bought our house in 1958—oh, it was a brand new build back then, beautiful hardwood floors that I swore I would never let get scuffed—I have kept a very strict routine. Mondays are for laundry, Tuesdays are for dusting, Wednesdays I do the floors... you get the picture. \n\nSo no, I'm not suddenly deciding to clean out the attic or reorganize the garage or anything out of the ordinary like that. The only thing is, like I told you, I just can't seem to sit down and relax when the chores are done! I'm just sort of stuck repeating my usual routine because my body just won't let me sit still and be peaceful. Does that make sense? He was always teasing me about it. We’d go to a dinner party—back when we used to do those sorts of things, oh, the sixties were a wonderful time for dinner parties, weren't they? I had this beautiful fondue pot... anyway, we’d go, and on the drive home, he’d just chuckle and say, \"Well, Margaret, you certainly kept the conversation lively tonight.\" \n\nBut no, my thoughts aren't racing. They just sort of hum along at their usual pace. I'm not having a million ideas popping into my head all at once, or feeling like my brain is moving too fast to keep up with. Maybe I talk a little more than some people, but it's not a new thing, and it's certainly not because my mind is running away with me. It's just... well, it's just me! I just think it's important to be clear, don't you? But no, no racing thoughts or fast talking here. Just the same old Margaret.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of time when you felt your mood was noticeably different, like more high-spirited, unusually cheerful, or even more irritable than usual, along with this restlessness or increased activity you’ve described?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doctor, I can't say that my mood has been any different at all. I haven't been unusually cheerful or high-spirited, and I certainly haven't been more irritable. I mean, I'm just... well, I'm just my normal self! Like I've been trying to explain, it's just this physical restlessness, this need to keep moving and doing my chores over and over. It has nothing to do with my mood. \n\nI'm generally a very even-keeled person, you know. Arthur always appreciated that about me. He used to say, \"Margaret, you're as steady as a grandfather clock.\" And it's true! I don't get overly excited about things, and I don't let things get under my skin easily either. I remember when the neighbors next door—the Johnsons, lovely people, they moved to Florida a few years ago—when they got a new dog, a little terrier mix, and it would bark and bark at the fence. Susan was just beside herself, saying we should call the homeowners association, but I just said, \"Oh, let the poor thing bark, it's just getting used to its new yard.\" \n\nSo no, I haven't had any strange moods or anything like that. I feel perfectly fine in my spirits. I just can't sit still! I'll be sitting there, perfectly content, maybe listening to a nice classical program on the radio, and my legs will just start to jitter and I have to get up. But I'm not upset about it, and I'm not thrilled about it either. It's just... bothersome, is what it is. Just bothersome. But my mood? My mood is exactly as it always is. Steady as a grandfather clock, just like Arthur said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or depressed, lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or had other symptoms of depression that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. Depressed? Heavens, no. I've never been one to get down in the dumps like that. I know some people do—my sister-in-law, Beatrice, Arthur's younger sister, she used to get terrible spells in the wintertime. She called it her \"February gloom.\" She would just pull the curtains shut and stay in her bathrobe until three in the afternoon, barely eating a thing. We used to take her over a nice casserole, usually a tuna noodle or maybe a chicken divan, just to make sure she was getting a hot meal. But I never understood it, myself. \n\nI've just never been built that way. I've always found plenty to keep me occupied and cheerful. Even after Arthur passed—and that was a terrible blow, of course, a terrible blow, we had been married for fifty-two years—I didn't sink into a depression. I grieved, certainly, I missed him terribly, but I kept my routine. I kept the house clean, I kept going to my garden club on Thursdays... oh, the garden club ladies were such a comfort. We were preparing for the spring flower show right around that time, and focusing on my hydrangeas really gave me something to look forward to. \n\nSo, no, I've never lost interest in the things I enjoy. I still love my gardening, I still love keeping a nice home, I still enjoy reading my magazines and listening to the radio. I don't feel sad or hopeless. I don't have any of those symptoms you're talking about. It's just this silly restlessness, this inability to sit still in my armchair when all the chores are perfectly finished. But my spirits? No, my spirits are perfectly fine, doctor. I've always been a very practical, optimistic sort of person, and that certainly hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was clearly different from usual—like feeling unusually high, overly confident, or irritable—along with this restlessness or pressure to keep moving that you've described?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit here, but no, I really haven't. My mood hasn't been different at all. I've never felt unusually high or... what was it? Overly confident? Goodness, no. I'm a very modest person, I like to think. I know my capabilities—I can bake a pie crust that flakes perfectly, and I can get a stain out of a white tablecloth like nobody's business—but I don't go around feeling, oh, grand or full of myself. \n\nAnd irritable? No, not irritable either. I told you about the Johnsons' dog, didn't I? I have a very long fuse, as Arthur used to say. It takes a lot to get me riled up. I just don't see the point in getting all bothered about things you can't control. \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any change in my mood to go along with this... this fidgeting. I truly don't know how else to say it, doctor, but no, not even for a single day.
